Vocabulary: reception: Area where visitors first report and are assisted. / visitor reference: Identifier linking a visitor to an arrangement. / host: Person responsible for receiving the visitor. / appointment record: Information describing a scheduled visit or meeting. / surname: Family name used to distinguish people with the same first name. / facilities: Workplace function associated with relevant building services. / sales: Business function associated with selling products or services. / scheduled time: Time recorded for the arranged meeting. / arrival: Visitor's appearance at the location. / check-in: Initial process for recording or verifying a visitor's arrival. / waiting area: Designated place where a person remains before being received. / host notification: Message informing the host that the visitor has arrived. / access clearance: Confirmed permission to enter a defined area. / unaccompanied access: Permission to enter or move without an escort. / escort: Authorized person accompanying a visitor where required. / visitor badge: Identifier issued under the relevant visitor procedure. / visitor log: Record of arrivals and relevant visit details. / restricted area: Space whose entry is limited by the applicable rules. / identity match: Agreement between the person or name and the relevant record. / appointment ambiguity: Uncertainty about which arrangement or host is intended. / reception wait: Period spent at reception pending the next arrangement. / host availability: Whether the intended host can presently respond. / arrival estimate: Expected arrival time that has not necessarily been confirmed. / visitor handoff: Transfer of responsibility for receiving the visitor.
Speaking task: Complete the reception dialogue. Read the welcome and waiting exchanges aloud. Keep V12, Jordan Chen in facilities, 10:00, and reception together. Complete the transfer and check the key. Reread the waiting instruction without inventing a host-arrival time.
Partner: Taylor: Visitor / Nia: Receptionist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the reception readback using the reference and the actual waiting arrangement.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Taylor: Could you repeat the full name and department once? I'd like to use the right surname.
Nia: Jordan Chen in facilities. Confirming the surname prevents confusion with Jordan Patel in sales. Your reference is V12, and you are waiting here at reception.
Taylor: Thank you. I'll stay here for the next update.
Nia: You are welcome. I will coordinate the visitor handoff once the host response is available. For now, the correct host has been identified and the waiting instruction is clear.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

07Clarifying travel options and booking statusA higher fare with a conditional refund+

Your goal

Compare two supplied rail quotes, explain the conditional refund amount, and distinguish a comparison from an approved booking.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A higher fare with a conditional refund

Two fictional rail quotes cover the same trip. Option A costs $70 and is nonrefundable. Option B costs $90 and permits a refund before departure with a $10 deduction. Traveler Mira's meeting is not yet confirmed, and purchasing approval is still required. Assistant Ben must compare the quoted terms accurately without choosing for Mira, promising a full refund, or treating the discussion as authority to book.

Mira: Traveler / Ben: Administrative assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the price difference between the quotes?

Answer and explanation

A. $20

Option B costs $90 and option A costs $70, so the difference is $20.

2. Under the supplied terms, what refund does B permit before departure?

Answer and explanation

C. $80

The $90 fare less the stated $10 deduction leaves an $80 refund before departure.

3. What must happen before purchase?

Answer and explanation

A. Obtain the required purchasing approval

Purchasing approval is explicitly still required before the assistant can book.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A costs $70 and is nonrefundable under these quoted terms. B costs $90 and permits a refund before departure less $10, leaving $80. B costs $20 more upfront; the $10 deduction is not the price difference.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
rail quote
Stated price and conditions for a proposed train journey.compare rail quotes
fare
Price charged for the specified travel.state the fare
nonrefundable
Not permitting a refund under the stated quoted terms.identify a nonrefundable fare
refundable
Permitting a refund subject to the applicable conditions.qualify refundable
deduction
Amount subtracted from a payment or refund.explain the deduction
before departure
Earlier than the stated leaving time.preserve the before-departure condition
refund amount
Sum returned under the applicable refund terms.calculate the refund amount
upfront cost
Amount paid at the time of purchase.compare upfront costs
price difference
Amount by which one price exceeds another.state the price difference
quoted terms
Conditions included in the supplied offer.preserve the quoted terms
booking status
Current stage of a travel reservation or purchase.clarify booking status
purchasing approval
Required permission to commit the organization to a purchase.obtain purchasing approval
unconfirmed meeting
Meeting whose occurrence or arrangement is not yet settled.identify the unconfirmed meeting
itinerary
Planned sequence and details of travel.check the itinerary
cash advance
Money provided before eligible expenses are settled, to be reconciled against the applicable claim.reconcile the cash advance
cancellation
Withdrawal from an existing booking under its terms.distinguish cancellation from comparison
refund eligibility
Whether the relevant conditions for a refund are met.check refund eligibility
fare condition
Restriction or permission attached to a ticket price.explain the fare condition
expense itemization
Separation of an expense into its component amounts and categories.check expense itemization
net refund
Amount returned after the stated deduction.calculate the net refund
travel authorization
Approval for travel within a defined scope.distinguish travel authorization
reservation
Arrangement holding travel space under specified terms.verify a reservation
ticket issuance
Creation or provision of the actual travel ticket.distinguish ticket issuance
conditional comparison
Explanation that preserves the conditions affecting the alternatives.give a conditional comparison

The $80 refund applies only under the supplied before-departure condition. Do not invent later refund rights, transferability, fees, or legal protections. The meeting is unconfirmed and purchasing approval is still needed; no ticket has been authorized by this comparison.

C Notice the language

Explain the condition as carefully as the price

The word refundable can hide a deadline and a deduction. State the upfront price, the condition, and the resulting amount together. A useful comparison helps an authorized decision without turning the assistant's explanation into a booking or financial recommendation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Define the comparison
Both quotes cover the same trip.
State option A
A costs $70 and is nonrefundable.
State option B
B costs $90.
Include the condition
B permits a refund before departure.
Include the deduction
The refund is reduced by $10.
Calculate the returned amount
Ninety minus ten leaves an $80 refund.
Compare upfront prices
B costs $20 more upfront.
Avoid a full-refund claim
Refundable does not mean the full $90 comes back.
Keep later terms open
No after-departure refund terms are supplied here.
Preserve meeting status
Your meeting is not confirmed yet.
Keep the choice separate
I can explain the options without choosing for you.
State the approval requirement
Purchasing approval is still required.
Avoid an accidental booking
This comparison is not a ticket purchase.
Distinguish the two amounts
The $20 premium and $10 deduction are different figures.
Request the next decision
Please confirm the authorized choice before any booking.
Close accurately
The prices and conditions are clear; the booking remains unapproved.
6 language and precision notes
Subject to
Introduces a condition that limits a general statement.
Before departure
Defines when the stated refund permission applies.
Less ten
Expresses the deduction from the ninety-dollar payment.
Upfront
Refers to the initial price rather than a later refund.
Not confirmed yet
Preserves the uncertainty about the travel purpose.
Before any booking
Keeps the approval decision ahead of spending.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is complete?

Answer and explanation

D. A is $70 nonrefundable; B is $90 with an $80 refund before departure

The comparison retains both prices and B's conditional refund after the deduction.

2. What does the $10 represent?

Answer and explanation

B. The deduction from B's refund

The supplied $10 is subtracted from B's $90 refund, not from the price comparison.

3. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. B is refundable after departure without restrictions

The supplied terms establish a before-departure refund only, with no later rule provided.

4. Which handoff is ready for the approver?

Answer and explanation

B. Both quotes with conditions, meeting unconfirmed, no purchase approved

A comparison can be passed for decision without selecting a fare, issuing a ticket, or extending the supplied refund terms.

E Conversations

A higher fare with a conditional refund

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mira: Traveler / Ben: Administrative assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • price difference
  • nonrefundable
  • fare
  • net refund
  • booking status
  • Purchasing approval
  • unconfirmed meeting
  • conditional comparison
  • before departure
  • upfront cost
  1. Mira
    My meeting isn't confirmed, so please don't book yet. Could you explain the two quotes for the same rail trip?
  2. Ben
    Yes. Option A has a 1 of seventy dollars, and option B is ninety. The important difference is not only the price but also the refund conditions.
  3. Mira
    A says nonrefundable. Is that the condition we're using for the seventy-dollar option?
  4. Ben
    Correct. A is 2 under these quoted terms. For B, a refund is permitted before departure, but ten dollars is deducted from the amount returned.
  5. Mira
    For B, how much would actually come back? Refundable sounds like ninety, but there's a deduction.
  6. Ben
    The 3 would be eighty dollars: ninety less ten. We should state the condition and the deduction together rather than simply describe B as refundable.
  7. Mira
    I nearly treated the ten dollars as the price difference. That's the amount retained from a refund, isn't it?
  8. Ben
    Exactly. The 4 is seventy for A or ninety for B. The ten-dollar deduction belongs to B's refund terms; it is not B's purchase price.
  9. Mira
    So B costs twenty more to buy, while the refund deduction is ten. Those aren't the same comparison.
  10. Ben
    Right. The 5 is twenty dollars. Keeping those two figures separate prevents a misleading comparison, especially when you are explaining the options to the approver.
  11. Mira
    What happens after departure? Does this quote actually say?
  12. Ben
    The supplied condition is 6. We have no later refund terms here, so I cannot extend the eighty-dollar calculation to an after-departure cancellation.
  13. Mira
    Please keep this as a comparison. Asking about refunds isn't an instruction to buy a ticket.
  14. Ben
    I will keep the 7 clear. This is a comparison, not a reservation or ticket purchase, and purchasing approval is still required before any commitment.
  15. Mira
    Can you give me a short summary for the approver without saying I've selected one?
  16. Ben
    Certainly. The 8 is A at seventy, nonrefundable; B at ninety, with an eighty-dollar refund before departure after the ten-dollar deduction.
  17. Mira
    Add that the meeting's still unconfirmed, please. That's why I'm checking the conditions.
  18. Ben
    I will preserve the 9 status alongside the quotes. The decision-maker should see the actual circumstances, not a message implying that the trip is already settled.
  19. Mira
    I'll get the choice and purchasing approval clarified. Nothing should be booked on the strength of this conversation.
  20. Ben
    Agreed. 10 remains outstanding. I will not issue a ticket or record an authorized choice until the required confirmation is available.

Answers and explanations
  1. fare Turn 2. Fare is the upfront travel price: seventy dollars for A and ninety dollars for B.
  2. nonrefundable Turn 4. Nonrefundable describes option A under the supplied quoted terms without inventing additional exceptions.
  3. net refund Turn 6. Net refund is eighty dollars after subtracting the stated ten-dollar deduction from B's ninety-dollar fare.
  4. upfront cost Turn 8. Upfront cost is the purchase price, separate from the amount retained if a conditional refund occurs.
  5. price difference Turn 10. Price difference is twenty dollars because the quoted fares are ninety and seventy dollars.
  6. before departure Turn 12. Before departure is the stated refund condition, and no after-departure permission is supplied.
  7. booking status Turn 14. Booking status remains unapproved because a comparison and questions do not authorize a purchase.
  8. conditional comparison Turn 16. Conditional comparison presents the two prices and B's limited refund without choosing or booking for Mira.
  9. unconfirmed meeting Turn 18. Unconfirmed meeting describes the unresolved travel purpose without converting it into a recommendation or cancellation.
  10. Purchasing approval Turn 20. Purchasing approval is the missing authorization required before Ben can turn the comparison into a booking.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mira: My meeting isn't confirmed, so please don't book yet. Could you explain the two quotes for the same rail trip?
  2. Ben: Yes. Option A has a fare of seventy dollars, and option B is ninety. The important difference is not only the price but also the refund conditions.
  3. Mira: A says nonrefundable. Is that the condition we're using for the seventy-dollar option?
  4. Ben: Correct. A is nonrefundable under these quoted terms. For B, a refund is permitted before departure, but ten dollars is deducted from the amount returned.
  5. Mira: For B, how much would actually come back? Refundable sounds like ninety, but there's a deduction.
  6. Ben: The net refund would be eighty dollars: ninety less ten. We should state the condition and the deduction together rather than simply describe B as refundable.
  7. Mira: I nearly treated the ten dollars as the price difference. That's the amount retained from a refund, isn't it?
  8. Ben: Exactly. The upfront cost is seventy for A or ninety for B. The ten-dollar deduction belongs to B's refund terms; it is not B's purchase price.
  9. Mira: So B costs twenty more to buy, while the refund deduction is ten. Those aren't the same comparison.
  10. Ben: Right. The price difference is twenty dollars. Keeping those two figures separate prevents a misleading comparison, especially when you are explaining the options to the approver.
  11. Mira: What happens after departure? Does this quote actually say?
  12. Ben: The supplied condition is before departure. We have no later refund terms here, so I cannot extend the eighty-dollar calculation to an after-departure cancellation.
  13. Mira: Please keep this as a comparison. Asking about refunds isn't an instruction to buy a ticket.
  14. Ben: I will keep the booking status clear. This is a comparison, not a reservation or ticket purchase, and purchasing approval is still required before any commitment.
  15. Mira: Can you give me a short summary for the approver without saying I've selected one?
  16. Ben: Certainly. The conditional comparison is A at seventy, nonrefundable; B at ninety, with an eighty-dollar refund before departure after the ten-dollar deduction.
  17. Mira: Add that the meeting's still unconfirmed, please. That's why I'm checking the conditions.
  18. Ben: I will preserve the unconfirmed meeting status alongside the quotes. The decision-maker should see the actual circumstances, not a message implying that the trip is already settled.
  19. Mira: I'll get the choice and purchasing approval clarified. Nothing should be booked on the strength of this conversation.
  20. Ben: Agreed. Purchasing approval remains outstanding. I will not issue a ticket or record an authorized choice until the required confirmation is available.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A cheaper fare with less flexibility

An assistant compares two rail quotes with a traveler.

  1. Assistant: For this journey, it's seventy nonrefundable, or a ninety-dollar refundable fare with conditions.
  2. Traveler: My meeting might move. What does conditional mean for the ninety-dollar fare you've found?
  3. Assistant: The supplied terms allow a refund before departure with a ten-dollar deduction, not a full refund.
  4. Traveler: That could matter. Does the seventy-dollar fare have the same cancellation option at a lower price?
  5. Assistant: No, that quote is nonrefundable. I haven't booked either, and purchasing approval is still needed.
  6. Traveler: Please send both options to the approver with those conditions, not just the headline prices.
  7. Assistant: I will. Is the arrival station the same one listed on your meeting invitation?
  8. Traveler: Yes, Central Station. I can reach the venue from there, but the meeting date remains unconfirmed.
  9. Assistant: I'll hold the booking until both the meeting date and purchasing approval are confirmed.
  10. Traveler: Thanks. Please contact me when the date and approval are settled, before buying anything.
A hotel date changed but the train did not

An assistant checks a colleague's itinerary.

  1. Assistant: Your hotel starts Wednesday, but the itinerary still shows Tuesday's outbound train.
  2. Traveler: The meeting moved a day. I changed the hotel, then forgot to ask about the train.
  3. Assistant: Let's check the existing train booking reference and its conditions before discussing a change.
  4. Traveler: The reference is T82. Please don't buy another ticket automatically while the original is still active.
  5. Assistant: I won't. I'll ask the travel desk what changes are available under that booking.
  6. Traveler: Could you keep the return date unchanged? The final session still ends on Friday afternoon.
  7. Assistant: Yes. The request concerns the outbound date only, with Friday's return remaining as currently booked.
  8. Traveler: The desk can review that today. Can you send me the revised itinerary only after confirmation?
  9. Assistant: I'll mark Wednesday's outbound journey pending until the travel desk confirms the change.
  10. Traveler: Good. I'll use the current reference for the query and wait before assuming the journey has moved.
A card statement is not the requested receipt

An assistant explains missing support for an expense claim.

  1. Assistant: Your expense claim includes the card statement, but the travel policy asks for the itemized hotel receipt.
  2. Traveler: I have a payment confirmation too. Does that show enough detail for this claim?
  3. Assistant: It shows payment, but not the room nights and separate items requested in this policy.
  4. Traveler: Then I need the hotel's itemized receipt. Can I ask them using my booking reference H39?
  5. Assistant: Yes. Quote H39 and the stay dates so they can locate the relevant document.
  6. Traveler: Please don't submit the claim as complete while I'm waiting for that missing receipt.
  7. Assistant: I'll keep it marked incomplete and note which document remains outstanding for the reviewer.
  8. Traveler: Could you check whether the train receipt is already included, so I don't request it again?
  9. Assistant: It is attached and readable. The hotel receipt is the only missing item identified here.
  10. Traveler: I'll ask the hotel for the receipt now and bring it back for checking.

Answer and explanation

A. seventy

2. Traveler: "Option B costs ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. ninety

Ninety is B's purchase price before any later conditional refund.

Answer and explanation

A. eighty

Eighty is ninety minus the stated ten-dollar refund deduction.

Language guidance: A costs $70 and is nonrefundable under these quoted terms. B costs $90 and permits a refund before departure less $10, leaving $80. B costs $20 more upfront; the $10 deduction is not the price difference.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both quotes cover the same trip. / A costs $70 and is nonrefundable. / B costs $90. / B permits a refund before departure. / The refund is reduced by $10. / Ninety minus ten leaves an $80 refund. / B costs $20 more upfront. / Refundable does not mean the full $90 comes back. / No after-departure refund terms are supplied here. / Your meeting is not confirmed yet. / I can explain the options without choosing for you. / Purchasing approval is still required. / This comparison is not a ticket purchase. / The $20 premium and $10 deduction are different figures. / Please confirm the authorized choice before any booking. / The prices and conditions are clear; the booking remains unapproved.
Vocabulary: rail quote: Stated price and conditions for a proposed train journey. / fare: Price charged for the specified travel. / nonrefundable: Not permitting a refund under the stated quoted terms. / refundable: Permitting a refund subject to the applicable conditions. / deduction: Amount subtracted from a payment or refund. / before departure: Earlier than the stated leaving time. / refund amount: Sum returned under the applicable refund terms. / upfront cost: Amount paid at the time of purchase. / price difference: Amount by which one price exceeds another. / quoted terms: Conditions included in the supplied offer. / booking status: Current stage of a travel reservation or purchase. / purchasing approval: Required permission to commit the organization to a purchase. / unconfirmed meeting: Meeting whose occurrence or arrangement is not yet settled. / itinerary: Planned sequence and details of travel. / cash advance: Money provided before eligible expenses are settled, to be reconciled against the applicable claim. / cancellation: Withdrawal from an existing booking under its terms. / refund eligibility: Whether the relevant conditions for a refund are met. / fare condition: Restriction or permission attached to a ticket price. / expense itemization: Separation of an expense into its component amounts and categories. / net refund: Amount returned after the stated deduction. / travel authorization: Approval for travel within a defined scope. / reservation: Arrangement holding travel space under specified terms. / ticket issuance: Creation or provision of the actual travel ticket. / conditional comparison: Explanation that preserves the conditions affecting the alternatives.
Speaking task: Complete the travel-comparison dialogue. Read both quotes aloud: A $70 nonrefundable; B $90, refund $80 before departure. Then read the separate approval requirement. Complete the transfer and check the key. Keep the $20 purchase-price difference separate from the $10 refund deduction.
Partner: Mira: Traveler / Ben: Administrative assistant

Optional second-round challenge: Complete the comparison using the supplied fares and terms. Do not turn the comparison into a booking.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mira: Add that the meeting's still unconfirmed, please. That's why I'm checking the conditions.
Ben: I will preserve the unconfirmed meeting status alongside the quotes. The decision-maker should see the actual circumstances, not a message implying that the trip is already settled.
Mira: I'll get the choice and purchasing approval clarified. Nothing should be booked on the strength of this conversation.
Ben: Agreed. Purchasing approval remains outstanding. I will not issue a ticket or record an authorized choice until the required confirmation is available.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Prioritizing work and handing over coverageEighty minutes of work in a sixty-minute window+

Your goal

Explain a capacity conflict with concrete timings and ask the manager to select a priority or change a deadline.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Eighty minutes of work in a sixty-minute window

At 10:00, assistant Rosa has a visitor list due at 10:30 and a room pack due at 11:00. Each requires thirty minutes. Manager Ellis requests a new twenty-minute spreadsheet check by 10:20. No colleague is available to help. Rosa must explain the conflict and ask Ellis to choose priorities or adjust a deadline. The case ends with that decision still needed; Rosa cannot truthfully promise all three original deadlines.

Rosa: Administrative assistant / Ellis: Manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is due at 10:30?

Answer and explanation

A. The visitor list

The visitor list has the earliest of the two original deadlines.

2. How much work is requested in total?

Answer and explanation

D. Eighty minutes

Two thirty-minute tasks plus a twenty-minute check total eighty minutes.

3. What should Rosa ask Ellis to do?

Answer and explanation

B. Choose priorities or adjust a deadline

The supplied capacity conflict requires a decision about priorities or timing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

At 10:00, the original two tasks already require the full sixty minutes until 11:00. The new check adds twenty minutes and is due at 10:20. Doing it first moves the earliest visitor-list finish to 10:50, beyond its 10:30 deadline.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
workload
Amount of work assigned or awaiting completion.explain the workload
capacity
Available ability or time to carry out work.state the capacity limit
priority decision
Choice about which task takes precedence.request a priority decision
deadline
Latest required completion time for a task.clarify the deadline
task duration
Time needed to perform the stated work.specify task duration
visitor list
Record of expected visitors for the relevant arrangement.prepare the visitor list
room pack
Set of materials prepared for use in the meeting room.assemble the room pack
spreadsheet check
Review of the specified spreadsheet information.perform the spreadsheet check
competing request
New demand that conflicts with existing commitments.identify the competing request
time window
Period within which work must be performed.define the available time window
sequence
Order in which tasks are carried out.agree the task sequence
trade-off
Consequence of choosing one priority at the expense of another.explain the trade-off
revised deadline
Newly agreed completion requirement.obtain a revised deadline
displaced task
Work moved later because another task takes its place.identify the displaced task
coverage
Availability of another person to handle work during the relevant period.confirm coverage
delegation
Assignment of a task to someone with the relevant availability and authority.avoid unsupported delegation
commitment
Promise to complete a defined action under stated conditions.make a realistic commitment
overcommitment
Acceptance of more work than the available capacity permits.avoid overcommitment
schedule impact
Effect of a change on task timing.explain the schedule impact
earliest finish
First possible completion time under the stated sequence and durations.calculate the earliest finish
existing obligation
Task already required before the new request arrives.preserve existing obligations
reprioritization
Change to the order or importance of assigned work.request reprioritization
handover coverage
Confirmed support for taking over specified work.verify handover coverage
decision readback
Repetition of the chosen priority and its consequences.prepare a decision readback

If the sequence is spreadsheet, visitor list, then room pack, the finish times are 10:20, 10:50, and 11:20. These are consequences, not newly approved deadlines. No colleague is available, and Ellis has not yet chosen which commitment should change.

C Notice the language

Make the trade-off concrete, not personal

Saying that you are busy does not show which commitment will move. State the existing deadlines and task durations, then ask for a priority decision. Respectful pushback gives the manager an accurate choice without silently accepting an impossible schedule or delegating to an unavailable colleague.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the request
I understand you need the spreadsheet check by 10:20.
Name the existing work
The visitor list and room pack are already due this morning.
State the first deadline
The visitor list is due at 10:30.
State the second deadline
The room pack is due at 11:00.
Give the durations
Each existing task needs thirty minutes.
Add the new demand
The spreadsheet check adds twenty minutes.
Show the capacity conflict
That is eighty minutes of work between 10:00 and 11:00.
State the immediate consequence
If I do the check first, the visitor list cannot finish before 10:50.
Keep consequences conditional
That sequence would also move the room pack to 11:20.
Avoid imaginary help
No colleague is available to take over a task.
Ask for a decision
Which task should take priority?
Offer the relevant alternative
Can one of the deadlines be adjusted?
Preserve prior commitments
I need to know which existing commitment should move.
Avoid vague assent
I cannot confirm all three original deadlines.
Request explicit agreement
Please confirm the revised priority or deadline.
Close without pretending
The schedule change still needs your decision.
6 language and precision notes
If I do ... first
Introduces a specific scheduling consequence rather than a refusal.
Cannot finish before
States an earliest completion under the given durations.
Would also
Extends the same conditional sequence to the later task.
Which should move?
Asks for a decision about the affected commitment.
No colleague is available
Removes delegation as an invented solution.
Still needs
Keeps an unresolved decision visible in the handoff.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. If Rosa does the check first, when can the visitor list finish at the earliest?

Answer and explanation

B. 10:50

Twenty minutes for the check plus thirty for the list gives a 10:50 finish.

2. If that sequence continues with the room pack, when does it finish?

Answer and explanation

A. 11:20

The check and two thirty-minute tasks require eighty minutes from 10:00.

3. Which response is professionally clear?

Answer and explanation

A. I need a priority choice or an adjusted deadline because these tasks require eighty minutes

The response states the capacity conflict and asks the manager for an explicit decision.

4. What remains unresolved at the end of the case?

Answer and explanation

C. Which priority or deadline Ellis will change

The case supplies the conflict but does not provide Ellis's final priority decision.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Eighty minutes of work in a sixty-minute window

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Administrative assistant / Ellis: Manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • priority decision
  • overcommitment
  • task duration
  • decision readback
  • schedule impact
  • competing request
  • time window
  • visitor list
  • revised deadline
  • coverage
  1. Ellis
    Could you check this spreadsheet by ten twenty? It needs twenty minutes, and I'd like it ready for the next discussion.
  2. Rosa
    Before I accept the 1, can we look at what's already due? It's ten now, and the existing tasks fill the next hour.
  3. Ellis
    I knew about the room pack. What's the other deadline?
  4. Rosa
    The 2 is due at ten thirty and needs thirty minutes. The room pack also takes thirty minutes and is due at eleven.
  5. Ellis
    If you start the spreadsheet right away, it finishes at ten twenty. Where does that leave the list?
  6. Rosa
    It uses twenty minutes of the 3. With the list next, its earliest finish becomes ten fifty, not ten thirty.
  7. Ellis
    Could you do the list faster to catch up? I'm trying to avoid moving the visitor deadline.
  8. Rosa
    The supplied 4 is thirty minutes. I can't responsibly promise a shorter one without a basis for it.
  9. Ellis
    Then the room pack would follow the list and finish at eleven twenty. That's late as well.
  10. Rosa
    Yes. That's the 5 of spreadsheet, list, then room pack. Those finish times aren't approved deadline changes yet.
  11. Ellis
    Could someone else assemble the pack while you work on the check?
  12. Rosa
    There's no available 6. No colleague is free, so a handover won't solve this particular conflict.
  13. Ellis
    All three feel urgent, but I see that saying urgent doesn't create another twenty minutes.
  14. Rosa
    I need a 7. Should the spreadsheet come first, and if so, which existing commitment may move?
  15. Ellis
    Let me check which deadline has flexibility before answering. I don't want you to drop the list without me realizing.
  16. Rosa
    Thank you. Any 8 needs explicit agreement. Until then, the list is still due at ten thirty and the pack at eleven.
  17. Ellis
    Don't treat the new request as fully accepted yet. I still need to choose what changes.
  18. Rosa
    Understood. That avoids 9: eighty minutes of work can't fit into the available sixty while preserving these original deadlines.
  19. Ellis
    Keep the durations and consequences in front of us. I'll use those to make the choice.
  20. Rosa
    Once you decide, I'll give a 10 of the agreed order and any changed deadline. For now, the priority choice is still outstanding.

Answers and explanations
  1. competing request Turn 2. Competing request is the new twenty-minute spreadsheet check that conflicts with Rosa's existing commitments.
  2. visitor list Turn 4. Visitor list is due at 10:30 and requires thirty minutes, so it occupies the first available half hour.
  3. time window Turn 6. Time window from 10:00 to 11:00 contains sixty minutes, less than the eighty minutes of requested work.
  4. task duration Turn 8. Task duration is thirty minutes for each existing task and cannot be silently reduced to make the schedule fit.
  5. schedule impact Turn 10. Schedule impact is a 10:50 list finish followed by an 11:20 room-pack finish if the spreadsheet goes first.
  6. coverage Turn 12. Coverage would require another available person, which the supplied situation explicitly lacks.
  7. priority decision Turn 14. Priority decision determines which task takes precedence and which existing commitment must change.
  8. revised deadline Turn 16. Revised deadline must be explicitly agreed rather than inferred from Rosa's explanation of the conflict.
  9. overcommitment Turn 18. Overcommitment would be promising all three original deadlines despite eighty minutes of work in the available hour.
  10. decision readback Turn 20. Decision readback follows an actual priority decision; no revised sequence or deadline is approved yet.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: Could you check this spreadsheet by ten twenty? It needs twenty minutes, and I'd like it ready for the next discussion.
  2. Rosa: Before I accept the competing request, can we look at what's already due? It's ten now, and the existing tasks fill the next hour.
  3. Ellis: I knew about the room pack. What's the other deadline?
  4. Rosa: The visitor list is due at ten thirty and needs thirty minutes. The room pack also takes thirty minutes and is due at eleven.
  5. Ellis: If you start the spreadsheet right away, it finishes at ten twenty. Where does that leave the list?
  6. Rosa: It uses twenty minutes of the time window. With the list next, its earliest finish becomes ten fifty, not ten thirty.
  7. Ellis: Could you do the list faster to catch up? I'm trying to avoid moving the visitor deadline.
  8. Rosa: The supplied task duration is thirty minutes. I can't responsibly promise a shorter one without a basis for it.
  9. Ellis: Then the room pack would follow the list and finish at eleven twenty. That's late as well.
  10. Rosa: Yes. That's the schedule impact of spreadsheet, list, then room pack. Those finish times aren't approved deadline changes yet.
  11. Ellis: Could someone else assemble the pack while you work on the check?
  12. Rosa: There's no available coverage. No colleague is free, so a handover won't solve this particular conflict.
  13. Ellis: All three feel urgent, but I see that saying urgent doesn't create another twenty minutes.
  14. Rosa: I need a priority decision. Should the spreadsheet come first, and if so, which existing commitment may move?
  15. Ellis: Let me check which deadline has flexibility before answering. I don't want you to drop the list without me realizing.
  16. Rosa: Thank you. Any revised deadline needs explicit agreement. Until then, the list is still due at ten thirty and the pack at eleven.
  17. Ellis: Don't treat the new request as fully accepted yet. I still need to choose what changes.
  18. Rosa: Understood. That avoids overcommitment: eighty minutes of work can't fit into the available sixty while preserving these original deadlines.
  19. Ellis: Keep the durations and consequences in front of us. I'll use those to make the choice.
  20. Rosa: Once you decide, I'll give a decision readback of the agreed order and any changed deadline. For now, the priority choice is still outstanding.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three deadlines cannot all stay the same

An assistant negotiates competing tasks with a manager.

  1. Assistant: Both original tasks take thirty minutes: visitor list by ten-thirty, room pack by eleven.
  2. Manager: Please do a twenty-minute spreadsheet check first, by ten-twenty. Can the other tasks still fit?
  3. Assistant: Not without delaying an existing deadline. No colleague is free; which commitment can we move?
  4. Manager: The visitor list must stay at ten-thirty. Could the room pack be finished a little later instead?
  5. Assistant: The training starts at eleven, so the trainer would need to agree to that delay.
  6. Manager: Keep both original deadlines. I'll accept the spreadsheet check at eleven-twenty, after the pack.
  7. Assistant: That gives each task the stated time. I'll tell you if a missing input changes the schedule.
  8. Manager: Please do. The spreadsheet can wait, but the visitor list must reach reception on time.
  9. Assistant: Visitor list first, room pack second, and your spreadsheet check by eleven-twenty.
  10. Manager: Agreed. I'll tell the person waiting for the check that I've accepted the revised time.
Coverage includes the phone, not every pending task

An assistant arranges lunch coverage.

  1. Assistant: Could you cover the reception phone from twelve to twelve-thirty while I take lunch?
  2. Colleague: Yes. Am I also taking over the supplier follow-up due at one, or only the phone?
  3. Assistant: Only the phone. I'll return before the one o'clock follow-up and keep responsibility for it.
  4. Colleague: Then please show me where the call-routing list is and which colleague handles urgent delivery questions.
  5. Assistant: The list is beside the phone. Facilities extension 214 handles delivery arrangements during that period.
  6. Colleague: I'll take messages and use that route if a delivery caller needs the team.
  7. Assistant: Thank you. There's a visitor expected at twelve-fifteen; their host knows to meet them here.
  8. Colleague: I can greet them and contact the host, but I won't change their room booking independently.
  9. Assistant: That's enough coverage. I'll be back at twelve-thirty and check any messages with you.
  10. Colleague: Fine. I'll keep the messages in the normal record and give you a short handover.
The missing list is holding up the room pack

An assistant gives a status update to a trainer.

  1. Assistant: The room pack is ready except for the final attendee list, which hasn't reached us yet.
  2. Trainer: I sent a draft yesterday with fourteen names. Can't you use that as the final list?
  3. Assistant: Yesterday's draft left two places unconfirmed. Has their attendance changed since then?
  4. Trainer: Those two have now accepted. I'll send the confirmed list of sixteen this morning.
  5. Assistant: Thank you. What time can you send it so I can prepare the correct number of packs?
  6. Trainer: By ten-fifteen. The session starts at eleven, and I need the packs delivered by ten-forty-five.
  7. Assistant: That works if the confirmed list arrives then. I'll prepare the unchanged materials while I wait.
  8. Trainer: Please avoid printing names from the draft if any corrections appear on the final version.
  9. Assistant: I will. The final list is the dependency; the other material can be prepared now.
  10. Trainer: I'll send the confirmed sixteen names by ten-fifteen and highlight the changed entries.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Eighty minutes of work in a sixty-minute window
  1. Complete the priority-negotiation dialogue.
  2. Read the proposed sequence aloud with finish times 10:20, 10:50, and 11:20. Identify them as consequences, not approved deadlines, using Rosa's supplied wording.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Ask for a realistic priority decision

Complete the workload readback. Keep calculated finish times separate from approved deadline changes.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Assistant: "The three tasks require ___ minutes in total."

Answer and explanation

D. eighty

Eighty combines the two thirty-minute tasks and the new twenty-minute check.

2. Manager: "The visitor list is originally due at ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 10:30

10:30 remains the original deadline until a change is explicitly agreed.

3. Assistant: "If the check comes first, the list finishes no earlier than ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 10:50

10:50 follows twenty minutes for the check and thirty for the visitor list.

4. Manager: "A priority ___ is still needed."

Answer and explanation

A. decision

Decision remains outstanding because no changed priority or deadline has been approved.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Office and Administrative Assistant English
Professional audience: Office assistants, administrative assistants, reception administrators, and team support staff
Scope: All organizations, records, prices, schedules, and policies are fictional. Use actual workplace approval, security, privacy, purchasing, and records procedures. The time equivalence is supplied for the fictional meeting date, not a permanent conversion rule. Travel conditions belong only to the quoted options. This book teaches communication, not financial or legal advice, and does not authorize purchases, access changes, payments, or visitor entry.
Lesson 08: Prioritizing work and handing over coverage
Original fictional case: At 10:00, assistant Rosa has a visitor list due at 10:30 and a room pack due at 11:00. Each requires thirty minutes. Manager Ellis requests a new twenty-minute spreadsheet check by 10:20. No colleague is available to help. Rosa must explain the conflict and ask Ellis to choose priorities or adjust a deadline. The case ends with that decision still needed; Rosa cannot truthfully promise all three original deadlines.
Language workshop: Eighty minutes of work in a sixty-minute window
Communication goal: Explain a capacity conflict with concrete timings and ask the manager to select a priority or change a deadline.
Language guidance: At 10:00, the original two tasks already require the full sixty minutes until 11:00. The new check adds twenty minutes and is due at 10:20. Doing it first moves the earliest visitor-list finish to 10:50, beyond its 10:30 deadline.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you need the spreadsheet check by 10:20. / The visitor list and room pack are already due this morning. / The visitor list is due at 10:30. / The room pack is due at 11:00. / Each existing task needs thirty minutes. / The spreadsheet check adds twenty minutes. / That is eighty minutes of work between 10:00 and 11:00. / If I do the check first, the visitor list cannot finish before 10:50. / That sequence would also move the room pack to 11:20. / No colleague is available to take over a task. / Which task should take priority? / Can one of the deadlines be adjusted? / I need to know which existing commitment should move. / I cannot confirm all three original deadlines. / Please confirm the revised priority or deadline. / The schedule change still needs your decision.
Vocabulary: workload: Amount of work assigned or awaiting completion. / capacity: Available ability or time to carry out work. / priority decision: Choice about which task takes precedence. / deadline: Latest required completion time for a task. / task duration: Time needed to perform the stated work. / visitor list: Record of expected visitors for the relevant arrangement. / room pack: Set of materials prepared for use in the meeting room. / spreadsheet check: Review of the specified spreadsheet information. / competing request: New demand that conflicts with existing commitments. / time window: Period within which work must be performed. / sequence: Order in which tasks are carried out. / trade-off: Consequence of choosing one priority at the expense of another. / revised deadline: Newly agreed completion requirement. / displaced task: Work moved later because another task takes its place. / coverage: Availability of another person to handle work during the relevant period. / delegation: Assignment of a task to someone with the relevant availability and authority. / commitment: Promise to complete a defined action under stated conditions. / overcommitment: Acceptance of more work than the available capacity permits. / schedule impact: Effect of a change on task timing. / earliest finish: First possible completion time under the stated sequence and durations. / existing obligation: Task already required before the new request arrives. / reprioritization: Change to the order or importance of assigned work. / handover coverage: Confirmed support for taking over specified work. / decision readback: Repetition of the chosen priority and its consequences.
Speaking task: Complete the priority-negotiation dialogue. Read the proposed sequence aloud with finish times 10:20, 10:50, and 11:20. Identify them as consequences, not approved deadlines, using Rosa's supplied wording. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the final request for the still-missing priority decision.
Partner: Rosa: Administrative assistant / Ellis: Manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the workload readback. Keep calculated finish times separate from approved deadline changes.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ellis: Don't treat the new request as fully accepted yet. I still need to choose what changes.
Rosa: Understood. That avoids overcommitment: eighty minutes of work can't fit into the available sixty while preserving these original deadlines.
Ellis: Keep the durations and consequences in front of us. I'll use those to make the choice.
Rosa: Once you decide, I'll give a decision readback of the agreed order and any changed deadline. For now, the priority choice is still outstanding.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Receipts are not the reimbursement

Reconcile an expense claim without paying company-funded or personal costs twice.

Fictional trip, all amounts in dollars. Hotel receipt $264, already paid directly by the company. Employee paid a $68 meal receipt: $48 eligible business share and $20 excluded personal share under this case's policy. Employee also paid eligible rail fare $42 and received a $50 cash advance. Figures include all applicable charges; no extra tax, fee, or currency conversion. Approval and payment are pending.

Mira: Traveler / Ben: Administrative assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • cash advance
  • out-of-pocket expenses
  • company-paid expense
  • itemization
  • payment method
  • net reimbursement
  1. Mira
    The receipts total three hundred seventy-four dollars. I took off the fifty-dollar advance and put three hundred twenty-four as the amount the company owes me.
  2. Ben
    We need to separate the 1 first. The two-hundred-sixty-four-dollar hotel bill was already paid directly by the company, not by you.
  3. Mira
    So the hotel receipt belongs with the trip record, but it isn't another two hundred sixty-four dollars to pay into my account.
  4. Ben
    Exactly. Keep it as a 2. Supporting the trip record and reimbursing you are different purposes; the receipt shouldn't disappear.
  5. Mira
    I paid the meal bill myself. It's sixty-eight in total, but the worksheet marks twenty as my personal share and forty-eight as eligible.
  6. Ben
    Use that supplied 3. Forty-eight enters this claim's eligible employee-paid amount. The twenty is excluded under the fictional policy.
  7. Mira
    And the forty-two-dollar rail ticket is fully eligible here. That gives me ninety dollars of eligible costs I paid, before the advance.
  8. Ben
    Yes. Those are the 4 relevant to this calculation: forty-eight plus forty-two. Don't add the meal's personal share back into that subtotal.
  9. Mira
    Now I subtract the fifty dollars I already received. That leaves forty, not three hundred twenty-four or ninety.
  10. Ben
    Correct. The 5 is applied once. It's not another expense and it doesn't need to be subtracted from every receipt separately.
  11. Mira
    Then the amount due to me is forty dollars, assuming this claim is approved. I shouldn't describe it as already paid.
  12. Ben
    Right. The calculated 6 is forty. Calculation, approval, and transfer of money are three different stages.
  13. Mira
    I still want the approver to see why the meal receipt says sixty-eight when the eligible line says forty-eight. Will that look like a mismatch?
  14. Ben
    Not if the split is clear. Retain the total receipt and show forty-eight business plus twenty personal. The two parts reconcile to sixty-eight.
  15. Mira
    And I shouldn't enter another tax allowance because these figures already include all the applicable charges supplied in this example.
  16. Ben
    Exactly. No extra tax, fee, or exchange adjustment has been specified. Adding one would change the case instead of checking it.
  17. Mira
    What if the advance had been a hundred, with the same ninety dollars of eligible employee-paid costs? Would the company still owe me ten?
  18. Ben
    The direction would reverse: ten would remain to return under the case's settlement rule. An excess advance isn't another reimbursement due to you.
  19. Mira
    I'll submit the hotel as company-paid, retain the meal split, include the rail receipt, and show ninety less fifty equals forty due.
  20. Ben
    That gives the approver a traceable reconciliation. Keep the personal exclusion and advance visible, and leave approval and payment marked pending until they're actually confirmed.

Answers and explanations
  1. payment method Turn 2. The hotel was paid directly by the company, while the meal and rail fare were employee-paid; adding all receipts does not give the amount owed to Mira.
  2. company-paid expense Turn 4. The hotel remains a documented trip cost but is excluded from employee reimbursement because the company has already paid it directly.
  3. itemization Turn 6. Itemization separates the meal receipt's forty-eight-dollar eligible share from its twenty-dollar personal share; the whole sixty-eight is not reimbursable in this case.
  4. out-of-pocket expenses Turn 8. The eligible employee-paid amounts are forty-eight for the meal and forty-two for rail, totaling ninety; the company-paid hotel is not added.
  5. cash advance Turn 10. The fifty-dollar advance is money already provided for the trip; subtract it once from ninety dollars of eligible employee-paid costs to obtain forty due.
  6. net reimbursement Turn 12. Ninety eligible employee-paid dollars less the fifty-dollar advance leaves forty dollars due to the employee, subject to the pending approval and payment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mira: The receipts total three hundred seventy-four dollars. I took off the fifty-dollar advance and put three hundred twenty-four as the amount the company owes me.
  2. Ben: We need to separate the payment method first. The two-hundred-sixty-four-dollar hotel bill was already paid directly by the company, not by you.
  3. Mira: So the hotel receipt belongs with the trip record, but it isn't another two hundred sixty-four dollars to pay into my account.
  4. Ben: Exactly. Keep it as a company-paid expense. Supporting the trip record and reimbursing you are different purposes; the receipt shouldn't disappear.
  5. Mira: I paid the meal bill myself. It's sixty-eight in total, but the worksheet marks twenty as my personal share and forty-eight as eligible.
  6. Ben: Use that supplied itemization. Forty-eight enters this claim's eligible employee-paid amount. The twenty is excluded under the fictional policy.
  7. Mira: And the forty-two-dollar rail ticket is fully eligible here. That gives me ninety dollars of eligible costs I paid, before the advance.
  8. Ben: Yes. Those are the out-of-pocket expenses relevant to this calculation: forty-eight plus forty-two. Don't add the meal's personal share back into that subtotal.
  9. Mira: Now I subtract the fifty dollars I already received. That leaves forty, not three hundred twenty-four or ninety.
  10. Ben: Correct. The cash advance is applied once. It's not another expense and it doesn't need to be subtracted from every receipt separately.
  11. Mira: Then the amount due to me is forty dollars, assuming this claim is approved. I shouldn't describe it as already paid.
  12. Ben: Right. The calculated net reimbursement is forty. Calculation, approval, and transfer of money are three different stages.
  13. Mira: I still want the approver to see why the meal receipt says sixty-eight when the eligible line says forty-eight. Will that look like a mismatch?
  14. Ben: Not if the split is clear. Retain the total receipt and show forty-eight business plus twenty personal. The two parts reconcile to sixty-eight.
  15. Mira: And I shouldn't enter another tax allowance because these figures already include all the applicable charges supplied in this example.
  16. Ben: Exactly. No extra tax, fee, or exchange adjustment has been specified. Adding one would change the case instead of checking it.
  17. Mira: What if the advance had been a hundred, with the same ninety dollars of eligible employee-paid costs? Would the company still owe me ten?
  18. Ben: The direction would reverse: ten would remain to return under the case's settlement rule. An excess advance isn't another reimbursement due to you.
  19. Mira: I'll submit the hotel as company-paid, retain the meal split, include the rail receipt, and show ninety less fifty equals forty due.
  20. Ben: That gives the approver a traceable reconciliation. Keep the personal exclusion and advance visible, and leave approval and payment marked pending until they're actually confirmed.
Keep the payer and advance visible

Another fictional trip has $120 of eligible employee-paid expenses, plus a separate $15 personal expense excluded by policy. The company directly paid $200 of additional travel costs. The employee received an $80 advance. No other amounts apply; approval is pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Traveler: Eligible employee-paid costs are ___ dollars.

Answer and explanation

A. 120

The case explicitly supplies one hundred twenty as eligible; the separate personal and company-paid amounts are not added.

2. Assistant: The advance to subtract once is ___ dollars.

Answer and explanation

D. 80

Eighty has already been provided to the employee and offsets the eligible employee-paid total.

3. Traveler: The calculated amount due to me is ___ dollars.

Answer and explanation

C. 40

One hundred twenty minus eighty equals forty; the personal fifteen and company-paid two hundred do not enter this reimbursement.

4. Assistant: The approval status is still ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. pending

Reconciling the amounts does not establish approval or payment of the claim.

Check the people behind the rows

Review a personalized mailing by recipient identity, selection rules, and field accuracy.

Fictional printed confirmation mailing: six rows, IDs A01, A02, A02, A03, A04, A05. The owner confirms the two A02 rows are an identical duplicate, not separate people. A01-A03 are confirmed; A04 declined; A05 is pending. Send one letter per confirmed person only. A02 and A03 share a postal address but are different people. A01's verified test postal code is 00123. Nothing has been dispatched.

Elena: Office assistant / Kai: List owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • recipient filter
  • leading zeros
  • recipient identifier
  • duplicate record
  • data source
  • merge field
  1. Elena
    The sheet has six rows, so I expected six letters. But A02 appears twice, and two different names have the same postal address.
  2. Kai
    Start with the 1, not the row count. A02 is duplicated; those two rows belong to one person, not two attendees.
  3. Elena
    That leaves five distinct people. Should I also combine A02 and A03 because their address matches?
  4. Kai
    No. A shared address isn't a 2 by itself. They're different people and each needs a separate letter if included in the approved mailing.
  5. Elena
    The mailing is for confirmed attendees only. A01, A02, and A03 are confirmed; A04 declined and A05 hasn't answered.
  6. Kai
    Then the 3 leaves three letters after resolving the duplicate. It doesn't leave five, six, or four with A02 counted twice.
  7. Elena
    I'll exclude the declined and pending records from this run. That doesn't mean deleting their records from the office's main list.
  8. Kai
    Correct. The 4 still contains their statuses. We're selecting a mailing audience, not erasing people or changing their replies.
  9. Elena
    The preview shows A01's postal code as 123, but your verified source says 00123. Can I just ignore the missing zeros?
  10. Kai
    No. Those 5 belong to the code. Preserve it as text and check the merged result against the source, rather than accepting a shortened identifier.
  11. Elena
    Changing the cell format now wouldn't recover characters already lost from an imported number. I'll check the original source value rather than invent padding.
  12. Kai
    Good. Also verify the 6 mapping. A correct greeting paired with another person's address would still produce the wrong letter.
  13. Elena
    I'll compare all three complete previews, not only the template. The shared address makes it particularly important to check the individual names.
  14. Kai
    Yes. A template can look right before actual values are inserted. Check each selected record's name, address, code, and confirmed status together.
  15. Elena
    The tool offers a duplicate finder based on names. Should its suggestion override your confirmed IDs?
  16. Kai
    No. A suggested match needs review against the record meaning. Our known duplicate is A02; matching names or addresses alone doesn't prove identical people.
  17. Elena
    So the check is three letters: A01, A02, A03. One A02, no A04 or A05, and no combined household letter.
  18. Kai
    That's the required selection. Keep the source correction traceable and confirm the final output count against those three identifiers.
  19. Elena
    I'll keep dispatch pending while we review the corrected previews. Generating a file hasn't put anything in the post.
  20. Kai
    Exactly. Record the list review and final output separately from dispatch. The result should be three accurate individual confirmations, not simply six copies because there were six rows.

Answers and explanations
  1. recipient identifier Turn 2. The supplied IDs distinguish people; the owner confirms that the repeated A02 row is an identical duplicate, leaving five distinct people in six rows.
  2. duplicate record Turn 4. A02 and A03 are different people sharing an address; only the repeated A02 row is confirmed as an identical duplicate in this case.
  3. recipient filter Turn 6. The confirmed-only selection includes A01, A02, and A03 once each; declined A04 and pending A05 are excluded from this particular mailing.
  4. data source Turn 8. The underlying contact data remains distinct from the subset selected for this mailing; excluding someone from the current run does not require deleting their source record.
  5. leading zeros Turn 10. The verified test code is 00123; dropping its leading zeros changes the identifier, and the prepared text value must retain all five characters.
  6. merge field Turn 12. A merge field inserts a particular source value into the template; the name, address, and code must all come from the intended recipient's record.
Read the complete conversation
